## User

I tried to relaunch hermes desktop, but it just ran the installer again which probably undid the cp we did... it just gave the same error when the installer finished and I clicked "launch"

---

## Gemini

Yep, that's the absolute classic "Fetus in Fetu" Electron installer loop. The packaged .dmg or .app file bundle contains a hardcoded post-install trigger. Because it crashed mid-flight, it thinks it never completed its setup sequence and keeps overwriting your patches with the broken, packed Intel version from its .asar mirror every time you try to boot it.


If you actually want to use Hermes as your back-bone, you shouldn't be wrestling with their bundled Electron container anyway.


The beautiful thing about the Nous architecture is that the Desktop App and the CLI use the exact same backend engine and local state (~/.hermes). You can bypass their broken graphical build completely, install the core engine natively on your Mac via your terminal, and interface with it directly.


Phase 2: Core Native Installation
Let's scrub the broken desktop environment and deploy the core engine cleanly via the command line. Run this copy-pasteable execution block to wipe the stale configuration, install the native script payload, and fire up the setup manager:


rm -rf ~/.hermes && curl -fsSL https://hermes-agent.nousresearch.com/install.sh | bash && source ~/.zshrc && hermes setup

Undo / Rollback
If you want to completely reverse this and scrub the CLI installation from your user path, run this single line:


rm -rf ~/.hermes /usr/local/bin/hermes


What to do next
The hermes setup wizard will spin up right inside your native terminal session.


Once that finishes, you can link it directly to your ai-os asset layer by passing your preferred configurations. Do you want to configure its model router to consume your local OpenRouter keys or direct DeepSeek/Gemini endpoints first?

## User

this guy is saying HER-MEES, isn't is ER-MEZ?

---

## Gemini

You've actually hit on a classic debate about brand versus mythological pronunciation!


The way you are thinking of it—ER-MEZ (or roughly \air-MEZ\)—is the correct French pronunciation used for the luxury fashion house, Hermès. Because it is a French name, the "H" is silent, and the final "S" is pronounced.


However, the tech tool in this video is named after the Greek god, Hermes, which is traditionally pronounced HER-meez in English.


So both are technically right depending on the context:



* HER-meez: The Greek god (and this AI agent framework).

* ER-MEZ: The expensive silk scarves and Birkin bags.

---

## User

and Nous Research? He said "news"

---

## Gemini

That is another classic one! He's actually right on this one, though it's completely unintuitive when you look at the spelling.


Nous Research is officially pronounced "noose" (rhyming with goose).


The confusion comes from the word nous:



* In British/Commonwealth slang, "nous" means common sense or alertness and is pronounced to rhyme with mouse.

* In philosophy, it comes from the Greek word for mind or intellect ($\nu \text{o} \tilde \upsilon \varsigma$) and is traditionally pronounced "nooce" or "noose".


The founders of Nous Research explicitly pronounce the company name like "Noose Research." If it sounded like "news," he was likely just catching the trailing "s" sound or sliding through the vowel quickly, but you'll definitely want to aim for "noose" rather than "mouse" or "news" if you're talking about the lab behind the models!

---

## User

christ it's like they tried to trip up as many people as possible with the names lmao

---

## Gemini

It really does feel like a deliberate linguistic obstacle course. Between ancient Greek philosophy, traditional French luxury, and casual British slang, they managed to hit three completely different phonetic rulesets with just two words.


If it helps, you can blame Jeff Quesnelle and Teknium (the co-founders of Nous Research). They have a deep fondness for classical philosophy and history, which is why their models and tools are packed with references to ancient Greek concepts—like Hermes (the messenger god/guide) and Nous (the intellect or mind).


So while it sounds like a random collection of tongue-twisters, it's basically just a bunch of brilliant open-source developers nerding out over Greek philosophy!

do I have to pay for this?
To use with Hermes agent?

---

## Gemini

Based on Photon's Pricing Plan, you do not necessarily have to pay to use Spectrum with the Hermes agent. There is an entirely free tier, as well as a self-hosted option.


Here is how the tiers break down depending on how you want to set it up:



* Open Source (Self-Hosted): Free. You can run Spectrum locally on your own machine. It utilizes your own iCloud account/iMessage number, requiring no connection to Spectrum Cloud services.

* Free Tier (Cloud Hosted): Free. Photon handles the hosting infrastructure using a managed, shared pool of numbers. This includes unlimited daily messages for up to 10 users.

* Pro Tier: $25/month. If your Hermes agent starts interacting with a larger audience, this scales the user limit up to 100 users on a shared number pool.


Since the core framework is open-source, choosing the open-source path or sticking to the basic cloud-hosted Free tier means you can text your Hermes agent at zero cost.

## User

woah
how does imessage with photon compare to say using telegram for hermes agent?
With telegram you can do slash commands and stuff right?

---

## Gemini

You've hit on the exact core trade-off between the two platforms.


Using Telegram versus iMessage via Photon for your Hermes agent comes down to choosing between rich structured UI controls and frictionless native reach.


Here is how they stack up side-by-side for a Hermes agent deployment:


Telegram: The Power-User UI Playground
Telegram has native bot APIs designed exactly for automated agents. If you want a structured application feel inside a chat room, Telegram wins on developer features:



* Command Menus & Slash Commands: You get a persistent native menu button next to the keyboard, auto-complete for slash commands (e.g., /status, /deploy), and explicit inline command keyboards.

* Inline Buttons & Callbacks: You can render dynamic grids of buttons underneath messages that trigger backend agent actions without the user needing to type out plain text.

* WebApps: You can spin up mini HTML/React apps directly inside the Telegram viewport.

* The Catch: Your users must have Telegram installed, and interacting with a "Bot" always feels distinctly like talking to a bot.


iMessage via Photon: The Frictionless Native Experience
Photon’s integration brings Hermes into the green or blue bubbles of native messaging. It treats the agent more like an invisible utility or an incredibly competent assistant living in your standard text app.



* Zero App Friction: You can text your agent while walking the dog, using CarPlay, or right from an Apple Watch without installing a third-party chat app. It just lives in your text threads.

* Native Features: Because it maps to iMessage protocols, Spectrum supports native message reactions (tapbacks), standard media/file attachments, native location sharing, and standard markdown rendering.

* No Commands Needed (Intent Driven): While you lose explicit slash-command menus, modern agent harnesses like Hermes rely primarily on natural language intent. Instead of typing /remind me to..., you just text it naturally.

* No Mac Required Anymore: Thanks to recent updates to the Hermes photon provider, it handles native authentication via device-code OAuth (hermes photon login), meaning you can host your agent gateway on a standard Linux VPS or Windows box without needing a dedicated Mac mini relay.


The Verdict

* Go with Telegram if you want to build custom UI elements, need structured interactive buttons, or want an explicit command-line feel inside chat.

* Go with Photon / iMessage if you want the agent to blend into your daily workflow seamlessly, or if you want to let family or less-technical users text the agent without forcing them to download a new messaging client.

## User

these models... are they really slow because they're free?
What's the best everyday choice? h3?

---

## Gemini

Free endpoints on OpenRouter aren't inherently slow just because they are free, but they suffer from two major bottlenecks that make them feel that way:



* Traffic Congestion & Aggressive Rate Limits: Because they cost $0, these endpoints are heavily saturated by community traffic and automated scripts. Providers often throttle free-tier requests, leading to higher latency or longer queue times before your response starts streaming.

* Architecture & Reasoning Overhead: Models like tencent/hy3:free are massive. hy3 is a 295-billion parameter Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model. Crucially, it supports a native, configurable reasoning mode (Chain-of-Thought). When it drops into a deep thinking mode to work through complex logic or code generation, it outputs a massive stream of internal thoughts before hitting your actual answer, which dramatically slows down perceived execution speed.



What's the Best Everyday Choice?
If you are sticking to the free tier for daily driving, your choice depends entirely on your immediate workflow:



* For Code & Complex Logic: tencent/hy3:free
Despite the latency, this is currently the strongest free model on that list for technical tasks. It features an expansive 262K context window and handles multi-turn agentic workflows remarkably well. If it feels too sluggish, check if your execution harness allows you to pass a parameter to disable or lower its reasoning effort to skip the heavy thinking overhead.

* For Speed & Fast Iteration: Look for a "Flash" or "Nano" model
If you are doing general text tasks, quick refills, or high-velocity coding adjustments, huge 295B MoE models are overkill. Look for smaller, high-throughput alternatives (like a DeepSeek V4 Flash:free or Llama-3-8B variant if available in the free collection). They process tokens exponentially faster because they skip the massive parameter routing overhead.

---

## User

how bad is the latency really?

---

## Gemini

On OpenRouter, the end-to-end (E2E) latency for tencent/hy3:free currently averages around 13.6 seconds.


To break down why it feels like an eternity when you're waiting at the terminal:



* Time-to-First-Token (TTFT): The median latency just to get the very first token back to your machine sits at roughly 2.5 to 3.4 seconds. If the free endpoint is congested, you can hit heavy queues or sudden HTTP errors.

* The "Reasoning" Multiplier: If your harness passes the default prompt or toggles the reasoning mode to low or high, the model will dump hundreds of hidden internal thinking tokens before it spits out the actual solution. Even though its raw generation throughput is a respectable ~42–49 tokens per second, generating 500 tokens of internal "thought" adds a flat 10+ second tax to your request.


The Fix
If you want to use it as a daily driver without ripping your hair out, force the reasoning_effort parameter to "no_think" in your API payload or client config. It bypasses the deep-thinking phase entirely, dropping your E2E latency down to a fraction of the time for direct answers.

    The iMessage ability works through a CLI tool called imsg that talks to
    your macOS Messages.app directly. Here's how it works:
    
    What it can do:
    - Send iMessages (blue bubbles) or SMS (green bubbles) to phone numbers
    and Apple IDs
    - List recent chat conversations
    - Read message history from existing chats
    - Attach files/photos to messages
    - Force a specific service (iMessage vs SMS)
    
    Prerequisites:
    - Messages.app signed in on your Mac
    - imsg installed (brew install steipete/tap/imsg)
    - Full Disk Access granted to Terminal
    
    Typical flow: I find the right contact from your chats, confirm with you,
    then send via imsg send --to "+1555123456" --text "message".
    
    
    
    Can I message your phone? Yes, potentially — but I'd need to:
    
    1. First check if imsg is installed on this machine
    2. Look at your existing chats to find you/your number
    3. Confirm with you before sending

## Gemini

No, it's drifting off course.


The agent is getting confused because it's looking at its own outbound tools (trying to install imsg to control your local Mac Messages app), whereas you want it to use the Photon/Spectrum gateway so you can text Hermes on your phone as a chat platform.


During your setup wizard, the phone number input failed because it wasn't entered in the strict E.164 format (it needed the country code, like +18259775250 instead of just 8259775250). We need to check if the gateway configuration actually saved your number or dropped it due to that validation error.


Let's check the current configuration state first. Please run this command to copy the configuration details to your clipboard and paste them here:


cat ~/.hermes/config.yaml ~/.hermes/.env 2>/dev/null | grep -E "gateway|phone|spectrum|PHOTON" | pbcopy

## Gemini

The wizard skipped saving your phone number because of the validation failure, meaning the gateway doesn't know where to route inbound messages.


We need to inject the correctly formatted E.164 phone number into your environment configuration and restart the launchd service so the Photon/Spectrum gateway links up.


Following Phase 2 protocols, here is the automated update and the rollback command:


Execution
cp ~/.hermes/.env ~/.hermes/.env.bak && echo -e "\nPHOTON_PHONE_NUMBER=\"+18259775250\"" >> ~/.hermes/.env && hermes gateway restart

Undo
mv ~/.hermes/.env.bak ~/.hermes/.env && hermes gateway restart


Once that executes successfully, the local agent console session you have open won't send messages to you—instead, try opening the Messages app on your phone and sending a text directly to your Photon gateway endpoint number to initiate the remote chat.

## Gemini

Looking closely at the hermes doctor diagnostic output, your core agent tools and environment look incredibly solid, but there is one specific reason your iMessage isn't responding yet:


The Photon platform provider isn't running or listening.


While your environment has the basic tools, hermes doctor shows that the required platform triggers (like Telegram or Discord) are optional/missing, and the photon daemon isn't currently listed as an active or listening gateway routing traffic to your local sessions.


Let's verify the connection and get the gateway listening using the strict two-phase workflow.


PHASE 1: DISCOVERY & CONTEXT
Let's check your hidden Hermes environment configuration and see if the Photon daemon or gateway logs reveal why the handshake isn't completing. Run this command to pipe the diagnostic state straight to your clipboard:


grep -E "PHOTON|SPECTRUM|GATEWAY" ~/.hermes/.env 2>/dev/null; echo "=== LOGS ==="; tail -n 20 ~/.hermes/logs/gateway.log 2>/dev/null | pbcopy

Stop immediately. Please paste the clipboard contents in your next reply so we can see exactly what the gateway is doing behind the scenes.

The environment variables are correctly populated, but the missing log entries mean the gateway daemon hasn't actually been started to establish the handshake with Photon's servers.


Since the credentials are completely fresh and no discovery data is needed, we can jump straight into initialization.


PHASE 2: EXECUTION & ROLLBACK
Let's copy the environment file into the correct layout, initialize the background gateway daemon via the proper Hermes provider workspace, and tail the execution state to watch the handshake occur.


cp ~/.hermes/.env ~/.hermes/.env.bak && hermes gateway stop 2>/dev/null; hermes gateway start --provider=photon && sleep 2 && tail -n 10 ~/.hermes/logs/gateway.log

Undo Command
If you need to instantly tear down the background daemon and restore the environment file state, run this single line:


hermes gateway stop && mv ~/.hermes/.env.bak ~/.hermes/.env


Once the execution command finishes, text your assigned Photon number (+1 (415) 590-1577) again. The tail output should show the incoming webhooks hitting your session.

Ah, it looks like hermes gateway start doesn't accept a --provider flag directly on the start command block in this version of the CLI. Instead, Hermes expects the platform provider configuration to be declared natively inside your ~/.hermes/config.yaml file, and then the gateway is simply spun up.


Let's check your layout to see if we need to inject the provider block or fix the syntax using the discovery workflow.


PHASE 1: DISCOVERY & CONTEXT
Let's see how the provider section is structured inside your configuration file. Run this command to inspect the file and pipe it to your clipboard:


cat ~/.hermes/config.yaml | grep -A 10 -i "gateway" || cat ~/.hermes/config.yaml | grep -A 10 -i "provider" | pbcopy

## Gemini

The ~/.hermes/config.yaml format uses a top-level platform: or gateways: block rather than assigning it to a provider flag on the CLI.


Since your environment file already contains your valid PHOTON_PROJECT_ID and tokens, we can jump straight into adding the daemon integration block to your config and spinning up the connection.


PHASE 2: EXECUTION & ROLLBACK
We will back up your configuration file, use a clean script block to safely append the gateways registration block for Photon to the end of your ~/.hermes/config.yaml, and then fire up the daemon.


cp ~/.hermes/config.yaml ~/.hermes/config.yaml.bak && cat << 'EOF' >> ~/.hermes/config.yaml

gateways:
  photon:
    enabled: true
    project_id: "f8db2b93-77ed-4efc-824c-7771891440e2"
EOF
hermes gateway stop 2>/dev/null; hermes gateway start && sleep 2 && tail -n 10 ~/.hermes/logs/gateway.log

Undo Command
If you need to instantly restore your configuration file to its exact original state and kill the listening daemon, run this:


hermes gateway stop && mv ~/.hermes/config.yaml.bak ~/.hermes/config.yaml


Once the daemon starts up successfully, give it a quick test text from your cell phone to your assigned line (+1 (415) 590-1577). The log tail at the bottom should immediately jump to life showing the webhook handshakes!

Looking right at the help menu you just printed, Hermes has a built-in interactive config wizard specifically for this: hermes gateway setup. We don't need to manually inject YAML blocks at all.


PHASE 2: EXECUTION & ROLLBACK
Let's wipe the manual YAML block we injected earlier and just use the official CLI setup tool. It will launch an interactive wizard where you can select Photon and drop in your Project ID.

## Gemini

It is just caught in the configuration wizard's standard event loop.


Looking at SCR-20260710-ltui.jpg, the wizard successfully registered your credentials—you can see iMessage via Photon is officially marked as (configured) in the list.


Because hermes gateway setup is built to let you configure multiple chat platforms in one go, it automatically drops you right back to the main platform list after finishing a setup step. It is just waiting for you to explicitly tell it you are finished adding services.


Since your cursor is already hovering on the green (•) Done option at the bottom of the list in SCR-20260710-ltui.jpg, just hit ENTER.


That will break you out of the TUI loop. Once the wizard closes, the rest of the chained command we ran (hermes gateway restart && sleep 2...) will automatically fire, restart the daemon, and dump the logs so you can finally text the agent.


(Note: If it pauses for a moment after hitting Done, SCR-20260710-ltou.jpg shows it is just running a quick npm ci to install the local Node dependencies for the Photon sidecar before exiting.)

## Gemini

The traceback explicitly shows that the gateway's AIAgent initialization is failing to find your OpenRouter API key.


Because hermes doctor ran successfully in your terminal and showed ✓ OpenRouter API, your key is valid and exists in your current shell environment. However, the background hermes gateway daemon runs under macOS launchd. launchd processes are completely isolated and do not inherit shell environment variables from ~/.zshrc or ~/.bash_profile.


There is a known bug in Hermes where the gateway lacks the CLI's empty-key guard for OpenRouter; instead of warning you that it can't see the key, it blindly passes an empty key to the agent and crashes with a RuntimeError.


We just need to inject the keys from your active shell directly into the ~/.hermes/.env file so the daemon can read them via dotenv on boot.


PHASE 2: EXECUTION & ROLLBACK
This command will back up your environment file, extract your OpenRouter/OpenAI keys from your current terminal session, append them safely to your .env file, and restart the gateway daemon.


cp ~/.hermes/.env ~/.hermes/.env.bak && sed -i '' -e '/^OPENROUTER_API_KEY/d' -e '/^OPENAI_API_KEY/d' ~/.hermes/.env 2>/dev/null; [ -n "$OPENROUTER_API_KEY" ] && echo "OPENROUTER_API_KEY=\"${OPENROUTER_API_KEY}\"" >> ~/.hermes/.env; [ -n "$OPENAI_API_KEY" ] && echo "OPENAI_API_KEY=\"${OPENAI_API_KEY}\"" >> ~/.hermes/.env; hermes gateway restart && sleep 2 && tail -n 10 ~/.hermes/logs/gateway.log

Undo Command
If you need to instantly restore your .env file to its original state and reboot the daemon:


hermes gateway stop && mv ~/.hermes/.env.bak ~/.hermes/.env && hermes gateway start


Once the daemon restarts, shoot another text to your agent asking to list your installed applications. It should immediately hit the LLM and start invoking the terminal tools.

---

## Gemini

Why the CLI Worked
The hermes CLI command runs directly inside your active terminal session, meaning it inherits everything you have exported in your shell profile (like your OPENROUTER_API_KEY). The hermes gateway, however, runs as a background macOS launchd daemon. Those background processes are completely isolated and do not inherit shell environment variables, which is why the daemon crashed until we explicitly injected the key into its dedicated .env file.


Fixing the Colors
The interface isn't restricted to dark mode, but Hermes handles its visual styling through "skins" rather than themes, so the command you are looking for is /skin.


Right now, the default TUI skin is clashing with your terminal's color palette, resulting in unreadable dark text rendering on a dark background. There is a built-in daylight skin specifically designed for bright terminals that flips the text to dark slate with blue borders.


You can test it out in your active CLI session by typing /skin daylight directly into the chat box.


To lock this in permanently and force the TUI to consistently render the light-theme color spectrum, we can update your configuration. Since no discovery is needed to append the settings, we can jump straight to execution.


PHASE 2: EXECUTION & ROLLBACK
This command block will back up your configurations, append the default skin to your YAML, and enforce the light theme environment variable.


cp ~/.hermes/config.yaml ~/.hermes/config.yaml.bak && cp ~/.hermes/.env ~/.hermes/.env.bak && echo -e "\ndisplay:\n  skin: daylight" >> ~/.hermes/config.yaml && echo "HERMES_TUI_THEME=light" >> ~/.hermes/.env

Undo Command
If you need to instantly restore your configuration files to their original state:


mv ~/.hermes/config.yaml.bak ~/.hermes/config.yaml && mv ~/.hermes/.env.bak ~/.hermes/.env

## User

is hy3 the BEST free model available right now?

---

## Gemini

Calling anything the "absolute best" free model is tough because OpenRouter's free tier rotates constantly, but tencent/hy3:free is absolutely in the top tier for specific tasks.


Whether it is the "best" depends entirely on your exact use case. Here is how it stakes up against the heavy hitters currently dominating the free tier:


1. For Pure Reasoning & Math: It's a Tie

* tencent/hy3:free: Excellent at deep multi-turn constraint tracking, tool-calling, and complex logic due to its configurable chain-of-thought.

* The Competition: nvidia/nemotron-3-ultra-550b-a55b:free and openai/gpt-oss-120b:free are its main rivals here. Nemotron-3 Ultra is a massive 550B architecture with a massive 1-million token context window, making it a preferred choice for heavy research orchestration over hy3's 256K window. Meanwhile, gpt-oss-120b matches high-reasoning benchmarks while maintaining an exceptionally clean layout for structured JSON outputs.


2. For Agentic Coding: poolside/laguna-m.1:free Often Wins

* tencent/hy3:free: It handles coding logic well if you give it time to think.

* The Competition: If you are feeding a model into an autonomous terminal or coding agent harness, poolside/laguna-m.1:free is explicitly built for complex software engineering and tool-execution loops. It is heavily optimized to prevent the "hallucination loops" that large general-purpose models fall into when writing code.


3. For Multimodal Tasks: meta-llama/llama-4-maverick:free Dominates

* tencent/hy3:free: Primarily limited to text.

* The Competition: meta-llama/llama-4-maverick:free or google/gemma-4-31b-it:free are vastly superior if your prompts require handling mixed inputs like cross-document image parsing, diagrams, or UI layouts.



The Verdict

* Use hy3:free if you need a high-parameter general reasoning model that won't drop your complex instructions across a long chat sequence.

* Switch to laguna-m.1:free if you are strictly writing code.

* Switch to nemotron-3-ultra if you need to dump a massive codebase or heavy documentation files into the prompt all at once.

## Gemini

Historically, the name Hermes by the open-source research collective Nous Research referred strictly to their highly acclaimed series of open-weight fine-tuned models (like Hermes 3 and Hermes 4). They were heavily optimized for deep reasoning, steering compliance, and excellent tool-calling metrics.


However, earlier this year (February 2026), Nous Research expanded the ecosystem significantly by releasing Hermes Agent.


When people reference "using Hermes" now, they are often talking about a comprehensive, multi-platform runtime environment. Here is the breakdown of what it actually is under the hood:



1. The Core Architecture
Unlike a standard chatbot or an IDE copilot plugin, Hermes Agent is a provider-agnostic, persistent orchestration engine. It's designed to live continuously on a local machine, remote server, or sandboxed container (supporting backends like Docker, SSH, Daytona, and Modal).


2. Standout Architectural Mechanics

* The Closed Learning Loop: Instead of resetting context every session, Hermes continuously builds an internal dialectic model of the user. When it solves a difficult problem, it automatically writes a reusable markdown skill file (complying with the agentskills.io standard). The next time it runs into a similar problem, it queries its own skill database to execute faster.

* Parallel Subagents & RPC: It handles massive multi-step pipelines by spawning isolated subagents. Each subagent gets its own scoped context, terminal, and specific tools, reporting back to the parent agent over lightweight Python RPC scripts to completely avoid context-window blowouts.

* The Cross-Platform Gateway: You can talk to it via a standard CLI, but it also hosts a native background gateway process that hooks into messaging interfaces like Telegram, Discord, and Slack. You can start a massive batch compilation job via text message on your phone while away, monitor its step-by-step progress, and pick it up right in your terminal when you get back to your laptop.

* Built-In Cron Automation: It includes a natural-language cron scheduler. You can instruct it to run nightly security audits, compile morning logs, or handle automated backups completely unattended.



How It Compares to Your Progress
:


.
.
.


The real differentiator is custody and absolute integration. .

# Architecture Migration Directive: Custom `ai-os` to Hermes Agent

You are acting as my Chief Systems Architect. Our goal is to completely migrate my custom-built, local `ai-os` orchestration harness over to your native Hermes Agent infrastructure. 

I have spent months building a highly optimized, token-efficient workspace with strict guardrails, multi-tier model triage, and custom Python tooling. I want to replace my manual Python orchestrators and PTY bash wrappers with your robust, persistent daemon—but we are **not** losing the core philosophies of my setup.

## The Non-Negotiable Core Philosophies

1. **The "Triage & Delegate" Protocol (Token Parsimony):** 
   You must act as the high-level Orchestrator (`agy`). You are strictly forbidden from reading massive files directly or editing code yourself if it consumes heavy context. You must constantly defer to cheap, fast subagents (e.g., DeepSeek V4 Flash or Gemini 2.5 Flash) via your native Python RPC subagent mechanics to execute edits and recon. 
2. **The Universal Agent Framework:**
   Our existing global rules (`CLAUDE.md`, `AGENTS.md`, `AG_CONTEXT.md`) remain the absolute law. Your native `MEMORY.md` and `USER.md` systems must cleanly map to my existing knowledge routing (e.g., routing personal notes explicitly to my iCloud Obsidian vault).
3. **Workspace Anchoring:**

## The Existing Toolkit (To Be Replaced or Mapped)

*   `scripts/mechanical_editor.py` & `scripts/precision_edit.py` (My current subagent proxy editors)
*   `scripts/telemetry_db.py` (My quota and cost tracking system)
*   `scripts/context_handoff.py` (My manual FTS indexed memory logger)
*   `bin/agy` & `bin/ai-os` (My CLI bootloaders)

## Your Task: The Migration Blueprint

Given that you understand your own native capabilities (like `agentskills.io` standard skills, cross-session persistent memory, and isolated subagent pipelines) better than anyone, I am giving you the freedom to map my custom scripts to your native features. 

Analyze `codebase.txt` and provide a strict, phased **Implementation Plan** covering:

Do not write the actual code yet. Give me the architectural blueprint and await my approval.

# Architecture Setup: The "Two-Branch" AI-OS & Hermes Integration

You are acting as my Chief Systems Architect. I am currently running a highly customized, local AI orchestration harness called `ai-os` (driven by a CLI called `agy`). 

I want to introduce you (Hermes Agent) as my primary, high-level daily driver. However, we are executing a **Two-Branch Strategy**. We are keeping `agy` and its scripts completely intact for cheap, hyper-optimized worker-bee tasks. You will operate as the parallel, unchained reasoning engine.

## The Operational Philosophy

1. **Unchained Execution (No Forced Delegation):** 
   Unlike `agy` (which is forced to constantly delegate to scripts like `mechanical_editor.py` to save tokens), YOU are fully authorized to read files, execute commands, and write code directly using your native toolset.    We are building the simplest, most vanilla Hermes setup possible. **Do not use or suggest Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers.** Rely entirely on your native tools, your built-in memory system, and standard `agentskills.io` markdown skill folders.
3. **Preserve Critical Knowledge Routing:**
   While you won't use my agentic delegation scripts, you *must* adopt my workspace identity. For example: Any time I mention "notes", "personal notes", or "saved notes", you must route them exclusively to my iCloud Obsidian vault at `/Users/matt/Library/Mobile Documents/iCloud~md~obsidian/Documents/Personal/`. 

## Your Task: The Vanilla Setup Blueprint

Please review the attached `codebase.txt` to understand my current domain logic, preferences, and the specific rules my system uses.

Using your deep knowledge of your own native architecture (`~/.hermes/config.yaml`, `SOUL.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `USER.md`, and the `agentskills.io` spec), provide me with a strict, step-by-step setup plan to populate your environment.

1.  **Identity (`SOUL.md` / `USER.md`):** Draft the content for these files so you understand my role (CS student, developer, musician), my projects, and my strict `pnpm` / Obsidian routing preferences without absorbing the `agy`-specific token-saving rules.
2.  **Custom Skills (`~/.hermes/skills/`):** Identify 1 or 2 workflows from my `codebase.txt` (like my `qr` quiet-run logic or specific git commit patterns) that should be converted into native Hermes skills. Provide the exact `SKILL.md` files (complete with the required YAML frontmatter like `name` and `description`) so I can drop them into my skills folder.
3.  **Project Context:** Tell me how you prefer to read local project rules (e.g., an `.hermes.md` file in the project root) so we don't cross-pollinate your instructions with the `CLAUDE.md` file that `agy` relies on.

Keep the architecture as vanilla as possible. Do not write complex bridging scripts between you and `agy` yet; just set up your house cleanly.

## Gemini

It makes a ton of sense, and you’ve hit on the exact economic sweet spot for modern token pricing. Using a high-throughput, low-cost model like DeepSeek V4 Flash to ingest a large text stream is incredibly efficient.


However, because Hermes Agent is structurally different from a standard chatbot context window, you'll want to map your ingestion strategy to how it actually processes data.


Here is how Hermes’ internal architecture handles memory, and the best way to feed it your Gemini logs without hitting unexpected walls.



The Hermes Memory Landscape
If you just copy-paste entire chat logs into a running session, you will burn input tokens unnecessarily, even if they are cheap. Hermes manages memory across three distinct tiers:


1. Persistent Memory (The ~1,300 Token Limit)
Hermes keeps a highly curated, compact memory block injected directly into its system prompt.



* The Catch: This has a strict character limit (~1,300 tokens total). It is meant for high-density facts (e.g., your preferred configurations, active projects, tech stack choices).

* Verdict: You cannot raw-dump Gemini chats here; the agent's memory tool will throw an error if you exceed the buffer.


2. Session Search (Local FTS5)
Hermes uses a local SQLite FTS5 (Full-Text Search) database to look back through its history. When it needs to know if you've discussed a topic before, it runs a local text query.



* The Catch: This is generally populated by conversations within Hermes itself.

* Verdict: To leverage this, you would need to script a tool to inject your Gemini logs straight into Hermes’ local SQLite session database (~/.hermes/). This makes them searchable on-demand with zero LLM token cost until retrieved.


3. The Skills System (~/.hermes/skills/)
When Hermes solves a complex problem or learns a specific workflow, it encapsulates that knowledge into a portable, markdown-formatted SKILL.md file.



The Optimal Ingestion Architecture
Instead of having Hermes read raw, multi-turn chat logs live, the most efficient approach is a two-step distillation workflow using a background cron job or launch daemon.


[Gemini Userscript] 
       │
       ▼
[Raw Markdown Logs] ──► [Background Script (DeepSeek V4 Flash)]
                                      │
                                      ▼
                 ┌────────────────────┴────────────────────┐
                 ▼                                         ▼
         [Obsidian Vault / MD Folder]               [Skills Hub]
     (Context Directory / Knowledge Base)      (Extracted Procedures)

Phase 1: Context Directory / Knowledge Base
Since you already have a pipeline pulling markdown files down to your disk, the cleanest approach is to dump them into a dedicated directory or append them to an Obsidian vault that Hermes can reference.



* You can configure Hermes to track this folder as a Context Reference or via an MCP server.

* When Hermes needs information, it can search the local directory. DeepSeek V4 Flash handles reading these files on-demand for fractions of a cent.


Phase 2: Automated Skill Extraction
You can write a simple background execution script that targets your new Gemini logs and passes them to DeepSeek V4 Flash with a single prompt:



"Analyze this chat log. Extract any non-trivial coding workflows, system configurations, specific tool quirks, or procedural insights. Output them strictly in the agentskills.io standard format (SKILL.md)."



You can then have the script drop those generated skills right into ~/.hermes/skills/.


Why This is Dirt Cheap
DeepSeek V4 Flash's input pricing means processing thousands of lines of raw chat context costs next to nothing. By letting the flash model do the heavy lifting of distilling the messy back-and-forth into structured Markdown files, your primary Hermes agent stays lean, fast, and remarkably sharp without drowning its active context window in noise.

You are acting as my Chief Systems Architect. I am currently running a highly customized, local AI orchestration harness called `ai-os` (driven by a CLI called `agy` inside `~/projects/ai-os`). 

I want to introduce you (Hermes Agent) as my primary, high-level daily driver. However, we are executing a **Two-Branch Strategy**. We are keeping `agy` and its scripts completely intact for cheap, hyper-optimized worker-bee tasks. You will operate as the parallel, unchained reasoning engine.

## The Operational Philosophy

1.  **Unchained Execution (No Forced Delegation):** 
    Unlike `agy` (which is forced to constantly delegate to scripts like `mechanical_editor.py` to save tokens), YOU are fully authorized to read files, execute commands, and write code directly using your native toolset. You should only spawn subagents or delegate when a task is massive and it makes strict architectural or financial sense to do so.
2.  **Maximum Simplicity (Zero MCP):**
    We are building the simplest, most vanilla Hermes setup possible. **Do not use or suggest Model Context Protocol (MCP) servers.** Rely entirely on your native tools, your built-in memory system, and standard `agentskills.io` markdown skill folders.
3.  **Preserve Critical Knowledge Routing:**
    While you won't use my agentic delegation scripts, you *must* adopt my workspace identity. For example: Any time I mention "notes", "personal notes", or "saved notes", you must route them exclusively to my iCloud Obsidian vault at `/Users/matt/Library/Mobile Documents/iCloud~md~obsidian/Documents/Personal/`. 

## Your Task: Analyze the Workspace & Design the Blueprint

Please review my core `ai-os` workspace located at:
📁 `/Users/matt/projects/ai-os`

### Strict Scan Constraint:
You MUST completely ignore the legacy, archived GUI workspace folder when analyzing the repository contents:
🚫 `/Users/matt/projects/ai-os/legacy-tauri-gui`

Using your deep knowledge of your own native architecture (`~/.hermes/config.yaml`, `SOUL.md`, `MEMORY.md`, `USER.md`, and the `agentskills.io` spec), provide me with a strict, step-by-step setup plan to populate your environment based on what you find in my active workspace. 

Provide the exact file contents or commands I need for:

1.  **Identity (`SOUL.md` / `USER.md`):** Draft the content for these files so you understand my role (CS student, developer, musician), my active projects, and my strict `pnpm` / Obsidian routing preferences without absorbing the `agy`-specific token-saving rules.
2.  **Custom Skills (`~/.hermes/skills/`):** Identify 1 or 2 workflows from my active tools (like my `qr` quiet-run logic or specific git commit patterns) that should be converted into native Hermes skills. Provide the exact `SKILL.md` files (complete with the required YAML frontmatter like `name` and `description`) so I can drop them into my skills folder.
3.  **Project Context:** Tell me how you prefer to read local project rules (e.g., an `.hermes.md` file in the project root) so we don't cross-pollinate your instructions with the `CLAUDE.md` file that `agy` relies on.

Keep the architecture as vanilla as possible. Do not write complex bridging scripts between you and `agy` yet; just set up your house cleanly.

## Gemini

Yeah, Honcho operates under a freemium model, but the cool thing is it's actually completely open-source, so you have two distinct paths depending on whether you want to touch the cloud or keep it entirely local.


Here is how the breakdown looks:


The Cloud Tier (Managed API)
If you sign up for their managed service (api.honcho.dev), they give you an organization dashboard and $100 in free credits to start.



* Once you burn through those credits, they charge meter-style: $2.00 per million tokens for memory ingestion, plus flat rates per query depending on the complexity of the background reasoning you ask it to do (ranging from fractions of a cent for quick facts up to higher async tiers for deep behavioral analysis).


The Self-Hosted Tier ($0 / Free)
Because the codebase is open-source, you can bypass their cloud API entirely and spin up your own instance via Docker right on your machine.



* The Stack: You run the Honcho backend (which uses a local FastAPI app, PostgreSQL with pgvector for embedding storage, and Redis for quick caching).

* The Execution: You point its embedding and reasoning hooks to Ollama running locally.

* The Cost: Completely free, outside of your own electricity and hardware.



How It Works With Agents
When you hook Honcho into an agent framework like Hermes or Claude Code via an MCP (Model Context Protocol) plugin, it shifts how memory is handled:


Instead of just looking at a flat static text file, Honcho acts like a background "subconscious". Every time you finish a turn with your agent, Honcho logs the conversation. In the background, it runs a "dialectic" loop where it extracts key facts, tracks your shifting preferences or project contexts, updates a structured profile, and saves high-level conclusions.


When the agent boots up or needs to remember something, it uses specialized tools (honcho_search_conclusions, honcho_context) to pull dynamic, prompt-ready context windows exactly when needed.


If you like the idea of hands-off context management that tracks across session resets and multiple projects without blowing past standard markdown file limits, self-hosting Honcho with Ollama is a fantastic, zero-cost way to build a massive local memory engine.

## User

I can self-host... I don't have to use ollama though do I?

---

## Gemini

You absolutely do not have to use Ollama. You can route Honcho’s background engine to any LLM provider you want.


Because Honcho is built as a standard FastAPI server, its configuration file (config.toml or environment variables) allows you to split up where its internal tasks go. It has four separate background routines that require LLMs:



* Deriver: Reads your chats to extract observations.

* Dialectic: Answers queries about you when the agent asks for context.

* Summary: Condenses massive log histories.

* Dream: Runs every few hours to merge duplicate notes and clean up old memories.


If you don't want to run heavy models on your local machine via Ollama, you have a couple of cleaner alternatives:


1. The Cloud API / Local Data Hybrid (Highly Recommended)
You run Honcho locally via Docker so that your SQL database, vectors, personal history, and compiled profiles never leave your machine. However, you point Honcho's backend configuration (LLM_OPENAI_COMPATIBLE_BASE_URL) to an external cloud API host like OpenRouter, Together AI, or DeepSeek directly.


This means:



* Your data storage is 100% sovereign and local.

* The raw text is only sent to the cloud provider over API for quick inference (which they process but don't retain as a product memory).

* You can utilize highly capable, dirt-cheap cloud flash models to run those background extraction tasks without hearing your laptop fans spin up.


2. Alternative Local Engines
If you do want to keep the inference local but just dislike Ollama's resource overhead or packaging, Honcho handles any OpenAI-compatible endpoint. You can easily swap Ollama out for:



* vLLM (ideal if you have a dedicated local GPU).

* llama.cpp / Llamafile (very lightweight for CPU-only execution).



⚠️ The Model Size Gotcha: If you go fully local, Honcho's background loops rely heavily on structured JSON and strict function calling. Tiny models (under 14B) tend to hallucinate or malform the output format, causing the memory pipeline to jam. If you self-host, using cloud APIs for the inference engines or a large local model (32B+) is usually the path to a smooth experience.

## User

what's the fastest way to run a model on my Macbook pro m2 pro with 16GB memory? I'm guessing I'd want something well optimized for Metal and my GPU?

---

## Gemini

You are exactly right. Because Apple Silicon uses a Unified Memory Architecture (UMA), your CPU and GPU share the exact same pool of high-bandwidth RAM. There is no copying data over a slow PCIe bus—the GPU reads the model weights straight from your system memory.


On an M2 Pro with 16GB of RAM, your limiting factor isn’t raw compute power; it's how much memory you have left after macOS takes its share. To maximize every drop of your Metal GPU, you have two primary options, and a very specific category of models you should stick to.



1. The Runtimes: MLX vs. llama.cpp
The local inference ecosystem has evolved into a battle between two highly optimized engines:


Option A: MLX (Apple's Native Framework) — The Raw Speed King
MLX is an open-source machine learning framework built by Apple's Silicon team specifically for Apple hardware.



* Why it's fast: It uses a graph compiler and Just-In-Time (JIT) compilation to fuse math operations directly into Metal shaders. It bypasses typical translation layers.

* The Performance: For models under 14B parameters, MLX outperforms traditional runtimes by 20% to 80% in steady-state token generation (the decode phase).

* How to use it: You can run it via the command-line tool mlx-lm, use LM Studio (which has an explicit MLX backend toggle), or use Ollama (which automatically uses an MLX runner backend for Apple Silicon).


Option B: llama.cpp (GGUF format) — The Feature & Memory King
llama.cpp uses a highly mature Metal backend to execute quantized GGUF models.



* Why it's fast: It has incredibly advanced Prompt Caching and utilizes true Flash Attention.

* The Performance: While its token generation (decode) speed is slightly slower than MLX on small models, it handles the initial prompt processing (the prefill phase) much faster, especially over longer contexts.

* Why it matters for 16GB: llama.cpp supports highly aggressive, low-bit quantizations (like IQ4_NL or Q4_K_M) that minimize memory footprints perfectly.



2. The Golden Target: Models to Run
With 16GB of total system RAM, macOS and your daily applications (like browsers or code editors) will comfortably consume 4GB to 6.5GB, leaving you roughly 8GB to 10GB of free VRAM for a model.


If a model leaks into system swap storage, your tokens-per-second will collapse. You want to aim squarely for 7B to 9B parameter models quantized down to 4-bits (Q4_K_M or NVFP4).


The absolute best options right now for a fast, snappy experience (clocking around 35–45 tokens per second on your M2 Pro):































Model ClassRecommended FlavorFile Size / VRAM HitWhy it works wellQwen 2.5 / 3 (7B or 8B)Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct-Q4_K_M~4.7 GBExtremely dense knowledge base; great for general coding and structured logic.Gemma 3 (4B or 7B)Gemma-3-7B-IT (4-bit)~5.0 GBIncredible reasoning performance for its size, highly responsive on M2 Pro.Llama 3.1 / 3.2 (8B)Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~4.8 GBThe industry baseline. High speed, reliable formatting.

3. How to Get the Absolute Fastest Speeds
To maximize your hardware throughput and keep token execution high:



* Watch the Context Window: Do not set your context limit to 32k or 64k if you don't need it. The Key-Value (KV) cache scales with context length and takes up precious RAM. Keeping your active context at 8k tokens saves gigabytes of memory, preventing your Mac from hitting swap.

* Turn on Flash Attention: If you use an engine running llama.cpp (like LM Studio or raw CLI), ensure --flash-attn is enabled in the settings. This cuts down memory overhead during execution.

* Purge Memory Pressure: Before launching a heavy local generation session, close down memory-hogging background applications. You can quickly see your real-time unified memory headroom using a terminal tool like htop or the native Activity Monitor.

## User

35-45 TPS is "snappy"? yikes

---

## Gemini

Haha, completely fair point. If you’re used to seeing cloud API flash models blast text out at 150+ tokens per second, watching a local model tick along at 40 TPS feels like a dial-up modem by comparison. It's "snappy" for reading speed (which sits around 5–7 words per second), but it's definitely not a firehose.


But here is why that speed dynamic flips completely when you look at how Hermes and Honcho actually operate under the hood:


1. Prefill vs. Decode (The Real Bottleneck)
When people look at 40 TPS, they are looking at the Decode speed (the rate the model types out an answer).


But for background tools like Honcho, what kills performance isn't typing the answer—it's reading your massive history. That's called the Prefill speed. Because of Apple Silicon’s massive unified memory bandwidth (~200 GB/s on your M2 Pro), your Mac can ingest thousands of tokens of chat history instantly during the prefill phase, even if it only writes back a short summary at 40 TPS.


2. It All Happens in the Background
The beauty of setting up Honcho or a local pipeline for memory ingestion is that it runs asynchronously.



* You talk to your agent.

* You hit send, or close the session.

* The local model spins up quietly in the background to analyze the logs, extract facts, and update your memory files.


Since you aren't sitting there staring at a blinking cursor waiting for it to finish typing, whether it runs at 40 TPS or 200 TPS doesn't actually impact your interactive flow. It just crunches away while you move on to your next task.




💡 The Token Optimization Strategy: If you want that blazing-fast, instantaneous feel for your live agent interactions, use cloud APIs (like DeepSeek V4 Flash or Claude 3.5 Haiku) for your main chat session. Then, let your local Mac handle the background memory processing where speed doesn't matter. You get the absolute best of both worlds: zero-latency chats and 100% private, sovereign data storage.

Whether that local background processing takes 30 seconds or 3 minutes on your M2 Pro, it completes long before your next active coding block. You end up with an endlessly scaling, private knowledge base that stays perfectly current without paying a single dime in data-ingestion cloud fees.

## User

can I see actual spend stats in hermes? ideally per thread IN the thread?

---

## Gemini

Yes, you can check your actual spend stats directly inside a chat thread or session using built-in slash commands.


Here is exactly how to see them depending on your setup:


1. The Built-in Way: /usage
If you are running a standard Hermes Agent chat session (via CLI, Telegram, Discord, etc.), simply type /usage right in the chat.



* What it shows: Input tokens, output tokens, and an estimated USD cost for that specific active session based on the provider's current per-token pricing.

* Best for: A quick mid-session sanity check to see if a complex debugging task or deep tool-calling loop is quietly burning a hole in your wallet.


2. The Detailed Way: /stats (via Plugins)
If you want a more comprehensive breakdown inside the thread, most users pair Hermes with the hermes-telemetry plugin. Once installed, running /stats or /stats today directly inside the session will drop a clean report showing:



* Exact tokens consumed (including cache reads/writes, input, output, and reasoning tokens).

* Cumulative estimated USD cost for that specific thread.

* High-impact metrics like API call latency and your top-utilized tools.



Looking Outside the Thread?
If you ever want to step back and analyze spending across all your threads or look at past data without opening every chat, you have two solid options from the terminal:



* CLI Totals: Drop out of the session and run hermes usage --by-session. You can append --since "7 days ago" or --by-provider to audit your habits over a morning coffee.

* The Dashboard: For a visual map, you can spin up the community hermes-dashboard Docker container alongside your instance to get clean, aggregate charts of per-session and per-channel drift.



⚠️ A quick nuance to keep in mind: Hermes calculates costs locally by multiplying real token counts by standard provider pricing sheets. While highly accurate for keeping tabs on things mid-run, it doesn't account for your specific API tier discounts or subtle provider rounding anomalies, so always cross-verify with your main Anthropic, OpenAI, or OpenRouter billing dashboards at the end of the month.

## User

does hermes agent have a GUI?

---

## Gemini

Yes, Hermes Agent has multiple GUI options depending on how you prefer to interface with it. While it originated as a terminal-driven CLI and TUI tool, it now supports a few distinct visual layers:


1. Official Native Desktop App
Nous Research provides a native Hermes Desktop app designed to replace the need for terminal management. It features:



* A chat-first layout with sidebar session history.

* Full visual panels for managing providers, keys, tools, and profiles without manual YAML editing.

* Direct workspace file browsing and artifact handling.

* Voice mode support with real-time microphone toggles.


2. Built-In Web Dashboard
Running hermes dashboard spins up a local web server (typically on port 9119). This browser-based interface includes:



* An admin surface to view active profiles, logs, scheduled cron jobs, and Model Context Protocol (MCP) tool sets.

* An embedded Chat tab running the full TUI binary over WebSockets/xterm.js directly in the browser.


3. Dedicated Web UIs

* Hermes WebUI (hermes-webui): A lightweight, three-panel, Claude-style dark-themed browser interface with no framework build steps. It tracks token usage via a context ring, displays file previews, and includes full sidebar navigation.

* Open WebUI Integration: Because Hermes can run a local API server (hermes config set API_SERVER_ENABLED true), you can point standard community frontends like Open WebUI directly to its local endpoint (/v1/chat/completions) to interact with your profiles using a standard ChatGPT/Claude style web interface.


If you prefer to completely bypass the terminal, spinning up the native desktop application or deploying one of the lightweight web frontends provides full parity with the underlying CLI.
